Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Crude rallies as market weighs supply impact of potential Middle East escalation
Trump says US not in talks with Iran
Houthis claim direct hit on Saudi Aramco Yanbu facilities
Middle East oil exports near 75% of prewar level
Crude oil futures settled higher Oct. 22 amid concerns that escalating Middle East tensions could impact supply.
NYMEX December WTI climbed $2.32/barrel to $99.47/b and ICE December Brent settled $4.35/b higher at $113.17/b.
Oil prices remained well bid, Warren Patterson, head of commodities strategy at ING Economics, and Ewa Manthey, commodities strategist at ING Economics, wrote in an Oct. 22 note.
The "sizeable risk premium currently embedded in prices is unlikely to fade until clear signs of de-escalation emerge and reassure investors that regional supply will remain uninterrupted," Patterson and Manthey said.
US President Donald Trump told reporters late Oct. 21 that Washington is not negotiating with Iran and that "something one way or another" would happen "fairly soon."
Vessel movements through the Strait of Hormuz were largely unchanged on Oct. 21, with 17 identified crossings compared with 16 a day earlier, according to a report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea, but transits through the Bab al-Mandab moved sharply lower, falling to 34 from 44 on Oct. 20.
Global Risk Management's Arne Lohmann Rasmussen said, "Attacks are no longer confined to Hormuz but are also affecting shipping in the Gulf of Oman. This increases risks to

International Crude
Middle East oil exports near 75% of pre-war level in mid-October: CAS
Middle East oil shipments reach 19 million b/d
IMO records 29 maritime attacks in October
War risk premiums rise to 12.5% of hull value
Middle Eastern oil exports rebounded to more than 19 million barrels per day by mid-October, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, despite continued Iranian attacks on merchant ships in and around the Strait of Hormuz.
Latest CAS data shows 15.6 million b/d of crude shipments from the Middle East on Oct. 12-18, or 82% of the average level between December 2025 and February 2026 before the outbreak of the US-Iran war. Total exports of petroleum products reached 3.5 million b/d, or 51% of the pre-war level.
On a seven-day moving average basis, Middle Eastern oil exports fell from nearly 21 million b/d on Oct. 1 to 15.2 million b/d Oct. 12 before the latest recovery to 19.2 million b/d on Oct. 18, equivalent to 74% of the pre-war level, according to CAS.
The figures comprise direct shipments from Persian Gulf ports via Hormuz to non-Middle Eastern locations, loadings from other tankers in the Gulf of Oman, Fujairah and Saudi Red Sea ports. They could be revised later as more data becomes available.
The recovery in oil flows came despite Iran launching its heaviest maritime attacks during the war, with the International Maritime Organization recording 29 attacks during Oct. 1-22, exceeding all monthly tallies since March.
Marcus Baker, global marine head of insurance brokerage Marsh, said additional war risk premiums were rising 10%-12.5% of a ship's hull value for Hormuz passage from 7.5%-12.5% as of Oct. 16.
Iran's Revolutionary Guards army struck four ships involved in a ship-to-ship transfer after sailing closer to Oman than Iran, leaving them on fire, it said in an Oct. 22 statement carried by state television. It also reported that three other ships struck mines on Oct. 21, although it was unclear whether they continued their voyages.
The UK Maritime Trade Operation Centre has reported one attack since Oct. 21. The British government agency earlier said it was "struggling to accurately report [the] true number of attacks with [the] majority of vessels attacked refusing to report to UKMTO."

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

"We believe the Houthis will continue to concentrate their efforts against the Saudis and the Yemen government forces as Saudi Arabia is continuing to increase its attacks against the Houthi positions," Ranslem told Platts in an email.

VLCC crossings The Strait of Hormuz had 17 ship transits on Oct. 21, with all
five VLCCs that made the crossing heading into the Persian Gulf, CAS analysts said in an Oct. 22 report.
The five VLCCs were identified as Saudi-linked Lawhah, South Korea's Sinokor-operated Mongolia Prosperity and Vietnam Prosperity, UAE-linked Arzanah and Caribbean Loyalty, according to CAS data. All but four of the 17 ships on Oct. 21 sailed into the Gulf, according to CAS. Traffic was up from 16 ships on Oct. 20. Arzanah completed a ship-to-ship transfer of Basrah Heavy crude near offshore Fujairah on Oct. 17 and has since entered the Gulf to load a new cargo, CAS said.
Bab al-Mandab traffic fell to 34 ships on Oct. 21 from 44 ships a day earlier, according to CAS. No VLCCs were observed for a fourth consecutive day.
While Yemen-based Houthi militants have promised to target only Saudi ships in the ongoing regional conflict, Dryad Global CEO Corey Ranslem said other ships sailing in the southern Red Sea face higher risks of collateral damage.
Meanwhile, ships operating in the northern Red Sea, Suez Canal and Eastern Mediterranean have continued to see electronic interference even though the Houthis have not targeted any ship there recently, the security consultant said.

-- Claudia Carpenter, Max Lin, Aresu Eqbali
Iraq's Basrah Oil, ExxonMobil sign MOU to advance 2.5 mil b/d project
Basrah Oil, ExxonMobil partner on seawater plan
Phase two targets 2.5 million b/d capacity
Iraq's state-owned Basrah Oil Company has signed a memorandum of understanding with ExxonMobil to implement the second phase of a project for the utilization of and investment in seawater, according to a statement issued by Iraq's ministry of oil and reported by the official Iraqi News Agency on Oct. 21.
"The MOU with ExxonMobil concerns the second phase of the seawater utilization project, which is designed to support oil fields and maintain reservoir pressure. The project will have a capacity of up to 2.5 million barrels per day, complementing another project currently being implemented by the French company Total, which has a capacity of 5 million b/d," Iraq's oil minister Basim Mohammed Khudair said, according to the statement.
"The project aligns with the government's and the ministry's strategy to attract international companies to develop the oil industry, ensure the sustainability of production operations and increase production rates," Khudair added.
The ministry maintains memoranda of understanding with several US companies, including Chevron and ExxonMobil, to leverage modern technology, he said, according to the INA statement.
"We look forward to continuing our collaboration with the government of Iraq to help unlock the country's energy potential through the application of ExxonMobil's technical expertise and commitment to safe, responsible operations," an ExxonMobil spokesperson said in a statement.
-- Ashok Dutta, Faleh Al-Khayat

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex sees one convergence
Cash differentials for sour markers rise further
19 Dubai partials trade during MOC
One Dec-loading Oman crude cargo declared
The Middle East sour crude complex saw one convergence during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 22, while cash differentials for key sour crude markers rose further amid ongoing geopolitical tensions.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai and cash Oman both at a premium of $8.09/b to same-month Dubai futures at the market close, up $2.55/b on the day.
December cash Murban was up $1.59/b over the same period at a premium of $8.09/b to same-month Dubai futures.
During the MOC, 19 December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were BP, Unipec, Mercuria and Mitsui, while the buyers were Trafigura, BP and Phillips 66.
BP declared a cargo of December Oman crude to Trafigura following the convergence of 20 partials in Platts cash Dubai.
A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.
-- Yong ren Toh

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
Differentials firm on shifting supply outlook
Oversupply hampers sour crude differentials
The North Sea crude oil market rose further Oct. 22 as expectations of late November WTI Midland arrivals began to shift, while transatlantic freight rates appeared to have found a floor.
Contrarian views on the end-of-November supply balance have begun to emerge, with renewed demand from Asia appearing to compete for WTI Midland cargoes.
"I think people are pausing a bit with freight rallying on paper," one Europe-based crude oil trader said Oct. 22. "November arrivals are feeling tighter as well. Some hidden demand, I think."
Yet another WTI Midland cargo changed hands during the Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 22.
A Vitol bid for a WTI Midland cargo arriving Nov. 16-20 was hit by bp at a premium of $12.90/barrel to Dated Brent.
Meanwhile, ENI sought a cargo arriving over the earlier laycans of Nov. 6-10 at a premium of $12/b to Dated Brent, but was unable to secure a seller by the London close.
The rebound in landed differentials of WTI Midland in Europe comes as transatlantic freight route rates stabilized from its selloff the previous week, with shipping market participants suggesting the rate has found a floor.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the US Gulf CoastUK/continent Aframax freight rate at $135.51/metric ton Oct. 21, down $1.06 from the previous session.
Market activity among native North Sea grades was heard to be thin, with one North Sea crude trader noting that there were no Forties offers heard in the market during the session.
The majority of second-half-November-loading Forties cargoes were heard to be unplaced earlier in the week.
Differentials in the regional sour segment continued to decline Oct. 22 as the ongoing supply glut in the European sour market dragged on prices.
A Johan Sverdrup cargo loading out of Mongstad Nov. 4-6 was offered by bp at a premium of $2.85/b to Dated brent. The offer demonstrated weaker value day over day and was left outstanding at the London close.
Apart from inflows of Middle Eastern sour barrels, competing grades from across the Atlantic have been limiting any potential upside for North Sea sour barrels.
"Hearing some Brazilian cargoes are hitting homes in Europe," a sour crude trader based in Europe said.
-- Ernest Puey

US oil rig count falls as fracking market remains tight
Permian rig count drops 6, down 12 yearly
Haynesville adds 5 rigs, leads basin gains
Frac crew market tightens with full calendars
The US oil and natural gas rig count fell by four in the week to Oct. 21, with oil-focused rigs retreating even as gas activity increased, and the broader count remained about 23 above year-ago levels, according to an S&P Global Energy CERA analysis published Oct. 22.
The Permian lost six rigs, leaving 239 active and flipping the annual trend, with the rig count down 12 compared to this time last year.
The Eagle Ford Basin was unchanged at 56 active rigs, up seven year over year, while the Bakken gained one to 37 active rigs, up five year over year.
The Denver-Julesburg Basin was up two week over week to 12 active rigs, unchanged year over year. Oklahoma's SCOOP/STACK play lost one, leaving 26, up just two year over year. Other basins, mostly oil-focused, lost four, leaving 144, but up five year over year.
Earlier this month, Crescent Energy purchased Devon Energy's Eagle Ford assets in a major $3.85 billion dollar deal on Oct. 8. Targeted to close this quarter or by early 2027, the acquisition includes some 68,000 barrels/day of oil equivalent of output -- around 40,000 b/d of net crude oil.
In its announcement, Crescent COO Jerome Hall said that Karnes Trough is among the lowest break-even oil resources in the Lower 48. Devon's recent wells demonstrate the potential of

October 22, 2026

these assets, and the company is expected to have six years of sub-$50/b and 10 years of sub-$60/b breakeven inventory, with an inventory life of over 15 years.
The Haynesville rig count was up five to 56 week over week, up 13 rigs year over year -- the largest year-over-year gain of any basin tracked in the data. Among other gas plays, the Utica lost one rig, leaving 11 and roughly on par year over year, while the Marcellus was down two to 16, lower by seven year over year.
Gas-directed horizontal rigs remain lower than their summer highs, and drilling activity in Appalachia and the Haynesville could continue to face downward pressure amid low gas prices.
Cash prices at several Appalachian trading locations have dipped below $1/million British thermal units over the past month, Platts data showed. At the same time, the Gulf Coast region is seeing increased supply as incremental takeaway capacity enables additional Permian gas volumes to reach the market, and a number of forecasters have been revising down Henry Hub price forecasts for 2027.
"Our view is a delayed call on incremental dry gas, not the permanent disappearance of that call," Scotiabank analysts said in an Oct. 22 note to clients. "We see a greater requirement in FY29+, while transportation, competing Texas demand and Louisiana deliverability determine how much Permian supply actually displaces Haynesville gas."
Even before the latest shoulder-season drop in gas prices, producers, including EOG Resources Inc. and Mach Natural Resources LP, had decided to drill fewer dry gas wells in 2026 than originally planned.
CERA data showed a frac crew count of 199 for the week ended Oct. 21, down from 203 the week before but up 24 year over year.
Liberty Energy CEO Ron Gusek said during an earnings update Oct. 22 that the average increase in rig count has ultimately supported higher demand for frac equipment, resulting in a relatively tight frac market.
"There is not capacity sitting around looking for work to complete. Our calendar is full. I think you'll hear from our peers probably over the course of the coming weeks that their calendars are full and utilization remains strong," Gusk said.
"I would have told you maybe a quarter ago that I wasn't sure that the fundamentals were there or clarity was there around 2027 yet. Now, as we've noted, we've seen the forward curve strengthen that hedge," Gusk added. "I think we have confidence in our customers' economics for the coming year, and I think that provides us a backstop for continuing to seek some modest additional price increases over and above the inflationary impacts."

Valero sees high tanker costs boosting USGC crude advantage
Freight surge widens crude discounts globally
VLCC rates to Asia jump sixfold from 2025
Heavy sour barrels flood discounted markets

Surging tanker freight rates have dramatically reshaped global crude trade flows, handing Valero's US Gulf Coast refining system a significant competitive advantage, as the cost of moving oil from the USGC to Asia has increased to as much as $35/barrel, company executives said in its third-quarter 2026 earnings call.
"The U.S. Gulf Coast continues to be one of the most advantaged regions globally, supported by abundant domestic crude production and access to cost competitive feedstocks," CEO Lane Riggs said on the Oct. 22 call.
The freight spike -- driven by ship congestion around the Strait of Hormuz, insurance risk premiums and transshipment delays -- is forcing crude sellers to widen discounts to offset shipping costs, making USGC barrels among the cheapest available globally and amplifying margins for refiners with access to nearby supply.
Valero's USGC advantage Randy Hawkins, who oversees crude supply at Valero, said on
the call that the USGC was "one of the most advantaged crude markets globally," and that the freight environment "only further supports that," signaling Valero expects the dislocation to persist long enough to materially benefit its refining margins in the near term.
Hawkins said Very Large Crude Carrier rates from the USGC to Asia have surged to $30-$35/barrel, compared with a 2025 average of roughly $5/b, according to Platts freight assessments from S&P Global Energy.
The cost of moving West Texas Intermediate crude from the USGC to Singapore on a VLCC has averaged $252.47/metric ton, or $34.44/b, so far in the fourth quarter, as of Oct. 22, compared to $95.47/mt, or $13.02/b, in Q3, according to Platts assessments.
The sharp rise is being compounded by ship-to-ship transfer operations in the Gulf of Oman, which are tying up global tanker capacity and adding transshipment delays and higher bunker costs to the overall freight burden.
The knock-on effect on crude pricing has been severe. Canadian crude discounts have widened to as much as $27-$30/ barrel under ICE Brent futures, as the prohibitive cost of routing Canadian barrels to Asia-based buyers has forced sellers to redirect supply toward closer markets at steep concessions.
Heavy sour grades from Latin America -- including Venezuelan and Mexican crude -- are also carrying a freight advantage into the USGC versus Asia, further expanding the pool of discounted feedstock available to Valero's USGC system.
"Looking ahead, our long-term outlook on crude differentials remains very constructive as incremental supply looks to be dominated by heavier and more sour barrels, especially out of Canada, Venezuela and the Middle East, which further supports the value of our complex Gulf Coast system," Hawkins said on the call.
Hawkins said Valero's USGC system remains the largest importer of Venezuelan crude, as it competes very favorably with heavy sour alternatives.
"In fact, in the third quarter, we saw the Venezuelan crude compete very favorably compared to other heavy sour

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

6

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

alternatives," he added. "And as a result, we set a new processing record for Venezuelan barrels in the Valero system during the quarter."
In its North Atlantic operations, Valero's exposure to elevated freight at its Pembroke refinery in the UK and, to a lesser extent, at its Quebec City facility was partially offset by the company's large fleet of time-chartered tankers, which provides a buffer against spot freight rate volatility, Hawkins said.
Valero's refining indicators reflect the impact on higher freight for its North Atlantic sector, on both the crude import and refined product export markets.
So far in Q4, Valero's North Atlantic indicators are averaging $29.55/b as of Oct. 22, down from the $47/b in Q3, according to the company's website.
Product export flows On the refined products side, higher freight is broadly a
tailwind for Valero's export economics, according to Gary Simmons, who oversees commercial operations.
"In our North Atlantic system, you generally have a nice uplift that we start to make Arctic diesel that carries some premium there," Simmons said. "Seeing some good tailwinds on the feedstock side with the heavy sours discounted and a lot of feedstock optimization opportunities."
When exporting into Latin American markets, Valero competes primarily against European-origin barrels, where the US refiner holds a structural freight advantage, Simmons said, adding that advantage widens as tanker rates rise, making Valero's cargoes more competitive on a delivered basis.
The one area of concern is diesel exports to Europe, where higher freight creates volatility in the transatlantic arbitrage.
Simmons said Europe remains structurally short of diesel and must attract imports to balance supply, meaning that when freight spikes, European diesel prices must rise to keep arbitrage economics viable for US-based exporters.
Valero expects freight-driven crude dislocation and its downstream effects on product trade flows are likely to remain a key variable for refining margins globally in the coming quarters, particularly if ship congestion around the Strait of Hormuz persists or intensifies.
Despite higher freight and market turmoil, Simmons said Valero was seeing no broad demand destruction despite higher fuel prices, with US wholesale gasoline and distillate sales up year over year and export demand strong. However, he warned that further crude price increases could squeeze refining margins.
-- Janet Mcgurty
Petroperu closes oil terminals due to rough seas
Peru harbor captains shut 75 Pacific ports
Petroperu's Talara, Salaverry oil terminals closed
Rough seas have closed ports for over 50 days in 2026

Rough seas have closed a total of 75 ports along Peru's Pacific coast, including state oil company Petroperu's main fuel terminals, the government said Oct. 22. Harbor captains have shut terminals in Talara, Salaverry, Chimbote, Pisco, Ilo and Callao, which handle most of the country's crude oil exports and refined fuel imports, according to Peru's Defense Ministry.
Adverse maritime conditions, which have coincided with warmer waters due to the approaching El Niño phenomenon, are expected to continue until Oct. 25, according to the ministry website. Unusually heavy seas have already closed Peruvian ports for over 50 days so far this year, hampering oil tanker loading and unloading operations, according to the government.
Petroperu's press office didn't immediately respond to an e-mail seeking comment.
Petroperu, which has been operating its 95,000 b/d north coast Talara refinery at 60% of capacity due to a lack of capital to purchase crude, signed a 16.2 million barrel crude import deal with Petroecuador this week after the government agrred to a $2 billion bailout.
At the same time, the company Oct. 22 reported it is working to contain an oil spill at its 200,000 b/d North Peruvian Oil Pipeline (ONP), the ninth break in the conduit this year. Petroperu, which competes with Repsol's 117,000 b/d La Pampilla refinery, reduced second-quarter domestic fuel sales by 22.2% from a year ago to 57,200 b/d, while exports fell 35.3% to 4,200 b/d. Crude purchases fell 47.2% to 32,800 b/d, while refined gasoline purchases fell 57.8% to 11,000 b/d.
-- Alex Emery
ARGENTINA DATA: September oil output rises to fresh record high
Output hits 973,700 b/d
Production rises 15.5% on year
Vaca Muerta shale leads rise
Argentina's oil production surged 15.5% year over year to a new record high of 973,700 barrels/day in September, while gas rose 3.2% to 143.1 million cubic meters/day over the same period, the Energy Secretariat said Oct. 21.
Crude production was up 4.5% from the previous record of 931,929 b/d in August, the department said on the social media platform X.
Vaca Muerta is leading growth, the department said, without providing production data for the huge play in northern Patagonia. In August, the play produced 668,414 b/d, or 72% of the national total, according to the latest data from the Neuquén Energy Ministry.
Gas production rises Meanwhile, Argentina's gas production rose 3.2% year over
year to 143.1 MMcm/d in September, the department said. September's output was down 7.9% from 155.4 MMcm/d in
August, according to the data.

Gasoline
Platts European Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Mediterranean gasoline tightens in November
High freight costs block ARA arbitrage flows
Blending components face sourcing challenges
European gasoline markets strengthened Oct. 22, though sentiment painted a tighter picture across the Mediterranean region amid elevated freight and high demurrage costs that were weighing on participants.
Platts assessed FOB AR Eurobob gasoline barges up $13.50/ metric ton day over day at $1,098.75/mt on Oct. 22, while the equivalent cash differential narrowed $3.50/mt to $54/mt.
Traders pointed to a tightening Mediterranean gasoline balance, particularly into the first half of November, with limited prompt availability and a growing need to draw barrels from Northwest Europe. However, high freight costs were said to be complicating the arbitrage from the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp hub into the Mediterranean.
"Med is getting tighter indeed, [and] needs to pull product from ARA," one European trader said, adding that gasoline spreads would need to strengthen quite a bit further for the arbitrage to become workable. However, gasoline stocks in the ARA hub drew 14.4% to 707,000 mt in the week ended Oct. 22, a multiyear low.
A second European trader similarly described the Mediterranean as tight in product and said the region would struggle without additional inflows from outside. "Freight is quite high," the trader said, adding that an easing in freight costs could provide relief to the market after gasoline cracks had already fallen sharply.
The tighter outlook was particularly evident for early November. A third European trader described the beginning of the month as "very tight," with fewer barrels available and more participants seeking additional volumes. The trader said the relative strength of ARA and the Mediterranean would determine where incremental barrels move, with the transatlantic arbitrage also competing for European gasoline.
"It's a battle between who rallies first, not who is collapsing first," the trader said of ARA and Mediterranean gasoline markets.

East Mediterranean buying was also supporting the regional outlook. One trader pointed to demand from Egypt, while another said demand was emerging across several markets and could lend near-term support to gasoline cracks, citing Syrian import requirements of around 90,000 mt/month, equivalent to roughly three cargoes.
Logistics remained a key constraint, with one trader describing freight as the "biggest hurdle" and citing expensive demurrage and costly delays despite softer backwardation. The same trader said the combination of elevated freight and declining gasoline demand was weighing on the market, while the recent pull from East Mediterranean markets could provide near-term support.
Blending economics remained another source of pressure. Traders said strong naphtha values had squeezed the economics of reformate production, while limited access to MTBE had increased demand for alternative octane boosters.
"The hard bit is to get your hands on blending material," one trader said, adding that gasoline blending demand had picked up over the preceding two to three weeks.
Another trader similarly described reformate and other blend components as difficult to source, with refiners incentivized to retain material internally. However, the source noted early signs of improving component availability and softer premiums for early November, with some material available inland in railcars and storage.
Demand signals were more mixed. One trader described Italian import demand as muted, saying rising CIF costs against relatively flat retail prices had made imports uneconomic, while weaker demand was also reported in the Adriatic, including Slovenia.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB AR Eurobob barge at $1,044.75/mt, up $17/mt on the day, with the equivalent crack down 15 cents/b at $18.50/b.
The November/December spread was up $5.25/mt at $40.25/ mt, with December/January up $3/mt at $22/mt.
The November Med/North gasoline differential - the spread between the November FOB Med swaps and the equivalent FOB ARA Eurobob barge - was up $1.25/mt at a $21/mt discount, with the December spread 50 cents/mt higher at a $22/mt premium.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Friedrich Gutsche

Japan subsidizes kerosene imports through March
Asian jet fuel/kerosene market fundamentals were largely unchanged Oct. 22, characterized by ample supply ahead of peak winter demand.

Brokers pegged the front-month November-December FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene swap time spread--an indicator of the near-term market outlook--at plus $1.61/b in midmorning Asia trading Oct. 22, unchanged from the Oct. 21 Platts Asian close assessment.
In the physical segment, the Platts FOB Singapore jet fuel/ kerosene cargo cash differential flipped back to a premium of 8 cents/b to the Mean of Platts jet fuel/kerosene assessments, up 12 cents/b day over day and snapping five straight sessions of declines. Still, the cash differential decreased by $3.74/b week over week.
Meanwhile, Japan will provide financial subsidies for the difference between kerosene import prices, including surcharge/ freight, and domestic wholesale prices, to refiners and oil product importers from Oct. 21 to March 20, 2027, according to a document distributed by the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry during an Oct. 20 briefing seen by Platts.
Refiners and oil product importers are required to keep supplying kerosene by the same amount in the same month of the previous year to receive the subsidies, the document showed. They are also required to apply to METI for subsidies and provide import and domestic wholesale prices. The definition of imports, such as the time of unloading or customs clearance, will be determined later, the official told Platts.
METI will calculate a monthly average of import and domestic wholesale prices for the subsidies, the official added.
The average weekly retail kerosene price in Hokkaido, Japan's northernmost region, rose 24.5% year over year to ¥155.70/liter (98 cents/l) Oct. 19, hitting a record for the second consecutive week, while the national average was ¥142.90/l, according to METI data published Oct. 21.
The statement said record-high wholesale prices raised the regional retail price above ¥190/l, higher than retail gasoline at about ¥170/l, due to a lack of imports, refiners' control of kerosene supply, and restrictions on kerosene supply from other prefectures.
Some South Korean refiners were heard to have concluded kerosene term negotiations with Japanese buyers, according to sources.
"Japanese buyers typically build up inventory at the cusp of winter to maintain safe inventories for their subsequent local heating sales program. Heating demand tends to be relatively price inelastic," said a trade source.
Japan Meteorological Agency's latest seasonal forecast as of Oct. 22 showed that the average temperature across Japan, excluding Hokkaido, over Oct. 24-Nov. 23 are expected to be higher than average. Tohoku has a 40% probability of experiencing above-average temperatures, while regions farther south have a 60%-70% likelihood of facing hotter-than-usual temperatures, according to JMA's forecast.
In spot tenders, South Korea's S-Oil offered 300,000 barrels of jet A-1 fuel for loading over Nov. 22-26 from Onsan via a tender

closed Oct. 21, with same-day validity. The award details could not be ascertained at the time of writing.

Diesel
Spain to release 4.4 million barrels of diesel, jet fuel reserves
Government taps private reserves until Dec 15
Middle distillate stocks drop to 82 days
IEA members accelerate emergency oil releases
Spain's government said Oct. 22 that it will release around 4.4 million barrels of diesel and jet fuel from its strategic reserves as part of the International Energy Agency's March 2026 Collective Action.
This is the second batch to be released by Spain, following a first batch of 3.75 million barrels in March.
The second release will include middle distillates - diesel, kerosene for aviation and other kerosenes, the Ecological Transition Ministry said in an order published in the State Gazette. The ministry did not provide a more detailed breakdown of the release.
The release into the market will take place until Dec. 15, the ministry said. The obligation will apply exclusively to stocks held by private owners, and does not affect the strategic stocks held by the Strategic Petroleum Reserves Corp., nor does it entail a reduction in their obligation to maintain such stocks, the order said.
Overall, Spain agreed to a total release of 11.5 million barrels, equivalent to 12.3 days of national consumption. Any further volume "will be released according to a schedule, volume, and composition determined by an ongoing assessment of developments in national supply conditions, stock levels, and the state of international markets, particularly for middle distillates, as well as analyses from the IEA and the European Commission," it said.
This second stock drawdown means Spain will reduce its overall reserve level of middle distillates from 92 days of demand to 82 days. Gasoline and fuel oil stocks will remain at 88 days, according to the document.
Spain's reserves are held between reserves corporation CORES, which usually holds 42 days of consumption reserves, and private operators.
The IEA Governing Board said Oct. 15 that its members will accelerate the release of emergency oil stocks, including more than 20 million barrels of diesel.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the European diesel benchmark ULSD 10 ppm CIF NWE Cargo price at $1,470.5/ mt on Oct. 21, down from a peak of $1,642.25/mt on Sept. 15, but more than twice the average of $714.72/mt in February.

Italian government approves additional diesel aid amid surging prices
Transport sector receives fuel tax credits
Diesel sales drop 8% amid price surge
Farmers get 45-cent per liter discount
Italy's Government said Oct. 22 it had signed an agreement with road haulage associations to release funds for the sector amid the recent surge in fuel prices.
"The Ministry of Transport has begun making payments, and in the coming days, companies will receive an advance on the tax credit for diesel covering the period from March to August 2026 in full," Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni said in a recorded statement.
Additionally, starting in November, the government will also "unlock outstanding payments for maritime transport, refinancing the tax credit for September-October 2026, adding 106 million euros to the 434 million already allocated in recent months, and extending the deadline for utilizing these credits until March 31, 2027, " Meloni said.
Other conditions requested by the associations will be implemented, such as the option to defer lease payments and a requirement to adjust certain contracts, particularly those offering less protection, to reflect fuel costs, Meloni said.
The government also approved measures to "support farmers and fishermen," including a 60 million grant to offset the cost of agricultural diesel. The measure will provide a discount of up to 45 cents per liter, covering more than 120 million liters. An additional 10 million will be allocated to the fishing sector, Meloni said.
"The agreement has been signed, and payments are already underway," Meloni said.
The measures presented Oct. 22 were a government response to widespread protests across Italy against rising fuel and living costs. Italy's Taranto refinery faced the threat of closure for at least a week due to union action and protests in the region.
Diesel sales down, gasoline sales up Italian oil product sales fell 2% year on year in September, to
4.31 million metric tons, according to data published Oct. 21 by Italian sector association UNEM.
The decline was led by diesel, with an almost 8% year on year decline to 2.06 million mt, the data showed. A drop in retail sales of around 5% was less significant than a decline of 9.4% in wholesale volumes, UNEM said.
Diesel is "feeling the price impact resulting from the Hormuz crisis more than others, an effect only partially offset by the reduction in excise duty, which expired on October 5," UNEM said.
By contrast, gasoline volume increased 5% year on year to 829,000 mt, while jet fuel volume rose 4% year on year to 508,000 mt.
Gasoline sales hit their highest September total for 16 years, while jet fuel volume hit an all-time September record, UNEM said.
The International Energy Agency said Oct. 15 that its members

will accelerate the release of emergency oil stocks, including more than 20 million barrels of diesel.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the European diesel benchmark ULSD 10 ppm CIF NWE Cargo price at $1,479 / mt on Oct. 22, up from $1,470.5/mt on Oct. 22, but still well below a peak of $1,642.25/mt on Sept. In contrast, the price averaged $714.72/mt in February before the Iran war triggered a major energy crisis.

Marine Fuel
China's Zhoushan overtakes Fujairah to become third-largest global bunker port
Zhoushan bunker supply reaches 8.54 mil mt in 2025
Singapore retains top spot with 56.18 mil mt
Asian ports gain amid shifts in global trade flows
China's Zhoushan overtook Fujairah to become the world's third-largest bunkering port in 2025, highlighting the growing prominence of Chinese ports in the global marine fuel market, according to rankings released at the ninth International Petroleum and Natural Gas Enterprises Conference in Zhoushan, Zhejiang province, Oct. 22.
Zhoushan supplied 8.54 million mt of marine fuel in 2025, placing it behind Singapore and Rotterdam but ahead of Antwerp-Bruges and Fujairah, according to the rankings.
Singapore maintained its position as the world's largest bunkering hub, with sales rising 3.8% year over year to 56.18 million mt. Rotterdam ranked second with 9.36 million mt.
Antwerp-Bruges ranked fourth at 7.62 million mt, while Fujairah slipped behind Zhoushan to fifth place with 7.27 million mt.
Zhoushan had ranked fourth globally in the previous ranking, closely trailing third-placed Fujairah, making its move into third place a notable shift in competition among the world's major bunker hubs.
Zhoushan strengthens bunker position Zhoushan's bonded bunker fuel sales rose 10.1% year over
year in 2025 on a comparable basis, according to data presented at the conference, extending growth at one of China's key international bunkering centers.
The port also supplied 515,500 mt of marine fuel to the domestic market during the year.
The world's 10 largest bunkering ports supplied a combined 116.7 million mt of marine fuels in 2025, up 3.3% year over year, indicating resilient bunker demand despite uncertainties surrounding international trade and financial policies.
Zhoushan was among several Asian ports to record higher bunker sales during the year, alongside Hong Kong, Busan and Shanghai. The rankings highlighted the growing importance of Asian bunkering markets as shifts in global trade flows and shipping routes reshape competition among major fueling hubs.

Resid
Platts European Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
VLSFO exports flow to Americas, Asia
European utility demand supports LSFO prices
HSFO market remains balanced amid low trade
VLSFO exports were supported by arbitrage windows Oct. 22, while HSFO fundamentals were balanced despite lower import volumes.
Arbitrage windows from Europe to the Americas and Asia were dictating fundamentals within the VLSFO market, participants said.
Availability of VLSFO and mid-sulfur product in Europe, combined with elevated premiums in Singapore, has meant that several vessels are leaving the region, providing support for the market.
"People are sending product to Asia on smaller vessels," said a European trader, adding that this was in order to combat the high Suezmax freight rates.
Despite the movement of volumes out of the region, the same participant observed ample blendstocks in the wider market, with product entering Europe from Brazil and Nigeria's Dangote refinery.
In the European LSFO market, utility buying appetite from Cyprus has supported fundamentals, despite entering the seasonally quieter winter period.
In the HSFO complex, the market was supported by lower import volumes from the Americas and reduced offers from East

Mediterranean production. Overall bunkering demand, however, has been subdued as the
higher flat prices weighed on end-user buying appetite, contributing to a balanced market across the European HSFO market.
"I would say fundamentals are balanced," said a second European trader.
In the Platts Market on Close assessment process, Spanish refiner Repsol bid for an HSFO cargo CIF basis Barcelona, but did not find a seller.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Tommy Petrou
Feedstocks
FOB Singapore bitumen prices extend gains even as supply expected to improve
Singapore bitumen prices rise to $835/mt
November supply boost may ease tight market: sources
Singapore's bitumen supply is expected to improve for November-loading cargoes, likely easing the tight availability that has driven FOB prices to record highs in recent weeks, several Asia-based traders told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in the week to Oct. 22.
Spot availability from Singapore refiners has remained constrained throughout October, supporting successive price increases amid limited cargo availability and firm regional demand. However, market participants expect additional volumes to become available for November loading, potentially offering some relief to regional buyers.
Singapore's bitumen exports fell 43% week over week to 4,217 metric tons in the week to Oct. 14, according to Enterprise Singapore data. Despite the decline, shipments remained 183% above the 1,490 mt recorded in the week ended Sept. 30, the lowest level since Platts began tracking the data in 2021.
Despite expectations of improved supply, spot prices remained elevated. Buying interest has remained cautious, with importers reluctant to commit to fresh purchases at prevailing price levels.
"If prices are still this high, I might just wait until December to buy," a Malaysia-based trader said.
The anticipated increase in November cargo availability could temper the upward momentum in Singapore bitumen prices, although the extent of any potential correction will depend on the volume of additional supply and regional buying interest, sources said.
Meanwhile, alternative supply sources across the region have also remained constrained, with reduced spot availability from South Korean refiners limiting export opportunities. In China, elevated domestic bitumen prices have reduced incentives for refiners to export material, resulting in fewer cargoes being offered to regional buyers.

Market participants are expected to monitor fresh Novemberloading offers and refinery availability for further indications of whether improving supply conditions will translate into softer spot prices.

CFR India bitumen prices rise on higher freight costs, tighter import supply
Freight costs rise to $350/mt from Middle East
Vessel shortages threaten construction supply
Imports drop 12.5% year over year
Indian bitumen importers are facing tighter Middle Eastern supplies and elevated freight costs as geopolitical tensions constrain vessel availability, just as the country's post-monsoon construction season begins, according to market participants.
Importers said securing vessels for shipments from the Middle East has become increasingly challenging amid disruptions along regional shipping routes, limiting their ability to import fresh cargoes despite expectations of improved seasonal demand.
"Finding a vessel is very difficult now. Even if material is available, getting a ship to move it is a challenge," said a trader based in Mumbai.
Freight rates for bitumen shipments from the Middle East to India's west coast have risen sharply in recent weeks, with rates reported at $350/metric ton, up from prewar levels of $60-$70/ mt, according to market sources. Elevated transportation costs, coupled with firmer offer levels from Middle Eastern suppliers, have increased the cost of securing imported material.
Shipping constraints have also raised concerns about the timely availability of imports, with traders and importers struggling to arrange new shipments from traditional Middle Eastern supply sources.
Meanwhile, India's post-monsoon construction season is underway, with roadbuilding and infrastructure activity expected to support a seasonal recovery in bitumen consumption.
"Demand has picked up as construction activity resumes, but the worry now is whether there will be enough material coming in," said an importer based in Kolkata. "With freight so high and vessels difficult to secure, we could face supply shortages in the coming weeks."
Market participants said a prolonged shortage of available vessels could further tighten domestic supply and bolster import prices.
India's bitumen imports fell 12.5% year over year to 168,000 mt in August from 192,000 mt a year earlier, according to data from

the Petroleum Planning and Analysis Cell. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed CFR West
Coast India VG30 and VG40 bitumen at $660/mt and $665/mt, respectively, on Oct. 21, both up $65/mt week over week. CFR East Coast India VG30 and VG40 were assessed at $700/mt and $705/mt, respectively, both up $65/mt from the previous week.

Platts assessed CFR North Asia propane and butane cargoes at $961/metric ton and $1,041/mt, respectively, Oct. 22, both up $40.50/mt from the previous session.

Platts assessed CFR South China propane at $968/mt Oct. 22, up $28.50/mt day over day.
Platts assessed the CFR North Asia propane-to-C&F Japan naphtha front-month swap spread at minus $26/mt Oct. 22, narrowing from minus $44/mt in the previous session.
In tender activity, Taiwan's CPC is seeking evenly split LPG for January-March 2027 delivery to Kaohsiung and/or Shen Ao, DES. The tender closes Oct. 27, with the validity until Nov. 3.
In the US, propane and propylene inventories fell below 106 million barrels for the first time since early August during the week ended Oct. 16, according to US Energy Information Administration supply and demand data published Oct. 21.
Stocks tightened by 500,000 barrels to 105.846 million barrels, their lowest level since early August, when stocks hovered at 104.974 million barrels, the data showed.
The US Gulf Coast accounted for nearly the entire stock draw, with regional stocks down 789,000 barrels at 65.939 million barrels. Other regions built inventories, including PADD 3, which built 398,000 b/d and PADD 1, which built 91,000 b/d, the data added.
Total demand, including exports and domestic product supplied, fell 149,000 barrels/day to 3.052 million b/d, according to EIA data. Of that total, exports rose just 40,000 b/d to 2.032 million b/d. Meanwhile, domestic product supplied fell 189,000 b/d to 1.020 million b/d.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Jeff Ong

Platts Western Mediterranean LPG Daily Commentary
Butane demand surges ahead of winter
West Med FOB butane rises $31/mt to $1,186
West Mediterranean LPG was firm Oct. 22 as seasonal demand picked up across the region.
Butane was the focus, with demand for North African and southern European cylinders building ahead of winter.
Sources described the Mediterranean butane market as short, with few prompt cargoes available. Sonatrach's elevated October pricing continued to anchor regional values, and expectations for November were for postings to stay firm given the Middle East supply outlook.
Turkey remained a key outlet for US-origin product, and sources said competition for Atlantic Basin cargoes between the Mediterranean, Northwest Europe and Asia was keeping delivered prices elevated. High freight and limited coaster availability were also cited as supporting regional premiums.
Overall sentiment in the West Mediterranean was bullish for butane and steady to firm for propane, with any deescalation in the Gulf or a fall in freight seen as the main downside risks.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the West Mediterranean FOB butane market at $1,186/metric ton, up $31/ mt day over day and stable versus naphtha at 127%, or at a $34/ mt discount to FOB Northwest Europe coasters.
Platts assessed coaster freight from Lavera to Mohammedia at $66/mt, steady day over day, based on market information received throughout the day.
The CIF Mediterranean butane large cargo market was assessed at $1,066.25/mt, or 114.25% of naphtha.
One deal was seen in the West Mediterranean FOB propane market. SHV Gas sold into a Vitol bid for a 2,000-mt cargo for Oct. 30-Nov.1 loading, pricing 100% Flat Price $930. This was deemed competitive and set the assessment.
Platts assessed the West Mediterranean FOB propane market at $930/mt, up $61/mt day over day.
The CIF Med propane large cargo market was assessed at $825.25/mt, or at a $17.75/mt premium to CIF Northwest Europe large cargoes.
In Morocco, Platts calculated the CIF very large cargo freight forward at $979.50/mt for delivery 45-60 days forward, down $7.50/mt.

A US agreement to resume imports of Russian diesel could disrupt Atlantic trade flows, although limited volumes and continuing attacks on Russian energy infrastructure are expected to temper the impact on the tanker market, analysts said.
The agreement reverses Washington's previous stance on Russian energy imports and introduces further volatility into an already complex tanker market, shipbroker SSY said in an Oct. 12 research note.
Europe's diesel deficit should continue to support US exports, but the return of Russian barrels could gradually alter the balance, analysts at S&P Global Energy Horizons said Oct. 22.
"MR rates may remain supported by Europe's diesel deficit and continued US exports, although improving prompt vessel availability and the resumption of Russian diesel flows could limit further gains in Atlantic employment," Horizons said.
The Platts Medium Range tanker index was assessed at $63,464/day Oct. 21, up from $60,540/day at the start of October and almost double its Feb. 27 level of $32,109/day.
The US remains a net diesel exporter, with shipments averaging 1.34 million barrels/day in 2026 to date, up from 1.17 million b/d in 2025, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. Key destinations include South America and Northwest Europe.
Russian diesel has flowed primarily to non-EU buyers in the Mediterranean and North Africa, including Turkey and Egypt, as well as to Brazil.
The US agreement was unlikely to have a significant effect on the wider market because of the limited volumes involved and continuing attacks on Russian energy infrastructure, SSY Senior Tanker Analyst Adam Lanning, told Platts Oct. 22.
EU, G7 and UK restrictions also remain in force, constraining the pool of shipowners willing or able to transport Russian products. Price-cap mechanisms continue to apply under those frameworks.
However, if Russian supply eases tightness in the US market, Washington could maintain exports to a wider range of buyers, the SSY Oct. 12 note said.

Charterers gain upper hand in WAF tanker market as rates correct
VLCC rates drop on scarce cargo inquiries
Suezmax tonnage builds for early November
WAF-UKC route falls w160 on week Oct. 21
The West Africa dirty tanker market softened across both the VLCC and Suezmax segments for the week beginning Oct. 19, as a lack of fresh cargo inquiries and a growing vessel supply list allowed charterers to push rates lower. After the extreme volatility seen in early October, the market entered a period of correction, with sentiment weighed down by weakness in adjacent markets and a sharply lower paper market.
VLCC market corrects on limited demand The VLCC market, which had firmed in the wake of the
Suezmax strength, began to correct this week amid a quiet Atlantic basin. A lack of fresh cargoes from the WAF region left the market largely untested, despite the recent period of elevated rates.
The downward pressure was confirmed by activity in the exBrazil market, where a fixture was concluded at a lower level after attracting eight offers. The softer sentiment in the Americas, combined with a weaker tone in the East, gave charterers the leverage to push for lower rates in West Africa. A lone market quote from WAF at the end of the previous week was covered at a softer level, establishing a new, lower benchmark. With limited demand and improving tonnage availability, the market remains under pressure.
Suezmax market searches for floor The Suezmax market also experienced a cooling of sentiment
after its recent uptick. The week began quietly, with no urgency

from charterers to fix as the tonnage list for early November began to build. The market was heavily influenced by a sharply lower paper market, which made charterers cautious on their counters as they waited for the physical market to follow.
Weakness in the VLCC segment also weighed on Suezmax sentiment, as softer rates for larger vessels limited charterers' appetite for Suezmaxes on long-haul voyages. With fresh inquiries remaining limited and the market largely untested on the physical side, rates remained fluid and under downward pressure. The market is now searching for a floor, with a fresh WAF-UKC test needed to establish a clear direction.
The downward pressure was evident in freight assessments. The Platts assessment for the WAF-Far East 260,000 metric tons VLCC route fell w123.5 week over week to w601.5 by Oct. 21. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
The benchmark WAF-UKC 130,000 mt Suezmax route also softened, dropping w160 to w742.5 over the same period.
-- Cresida Rodrigues
Suez tanker transits at three-year high on Saudi crude rerouting: CAS
More Saudi crude flows through Egypt
Tanker rates soar on longer Asian routes
Container ship transits also near 2023 level
Tanker traffic through the Suez Canal has risen to the highest in nearly three years on increased Saudi crude flows, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, while canal authority officials are discussing with leading liners on increasing container volumes.
CAS data shows weekly crossings of oil and LPG tankers reached 143 in the week ended Oct. 15, having amounted to the same level in the week ended Sept. 24, and 146 in the week ended Sept. 10, having averaged 106 year to date in 2026.
The crossings seen in recent weeks were the highest since December 2023, when weekly transits started to collapse from a normal level of 150-160 as Houthi militants launched intense attacks on merchant ships in the Red Sea as part of the IsraelHamas war.
Having ramped down their maritime attacks in 2024, the Yemen-based Houthi group declared a maritime blockade on all Saudi-linked shipping on July 20 and have since then targeted some Bahri tankers while refraining from targeting Western ships.
Suez traffic increase "closely coincides with escalating Houthi attacks targeting Saudi shipping near the Bab al-Mandab strait," CAS analysts said in an Oct. 20 report.
Overall, northbound crude flows via the Egyptian canal averaged 944,000 barrels per day in August, 949,000 b/d in September, and 924,000 b/d in October, to date, rising considerably higher than an average of 60,000 b/d between March and May, CAS analysts said, attributing the increase to a boost in Saudi crude flows.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

Saudi Arabia has increased exports from its Red Sea coast since the outbreak of the Middle East war at the end of February, with heightened security risks around the Strait of Hormuz. Latest Houthi threats have prompted more Saudi shipments via Suez rather than Bab al-Mandab, according to CAS.
Saudi crude shipments moving north through the Suez reached about 653,000 b/d Oct. 1-19, according to CAS data, accounting for the majority of northbound crude flows. Red Seaorigin crude makes up more than 70% of northbound volumes since July, while the remaining 30% originates from the Middle East Gulf, CAS analysts said.
Longer journey Saudi Arabia has also increased its use of Egypt's SUMED
pipeline, which carries crude across Egypt for re-export via the Mediterranean-facing Sidi Kerir terminal. Saudi crude deliveries to the Red Sea Ain Sukhna terminal rose to 1.113 million b/d in October to date, according to CAS, while loadings from Sidi Kerir reached 1.128 million b/d.
While Saudi crude flows from Sidi Kerir typically landed at European refineries pre-war, around 663,000 b/d of Arab crude loaded at the Mediterranean terminal this month, to date, was bound for Asia, despite the longer journey, according to CAS.
A Yanbu-Singapore voyage takes about 18 days when passing through the Bab al-Mandab strait, according to CAS, while it would take more than 45 days to ship via Suez and the Cape of Good Hope.
The longer voyages have tightened tanker availability and raised freight costs, CAS reported. Platts' Yanbu-Far East VLCC freight rate assessment hit a record $214.98/mt on Oct. 7 from $80.56/mt on Sept. 1. Platts' Dirty Sidi Kerir-Far East VLCC freight assessment reached $264.81/mt on Oct. 19, up from $185.19/mt upon its debut last month, due to "intense demand for tonnage on these alternative routes," according to the CAS report. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Overall improvement A total of 345 ships of all types transited the Suez Canal in
the week ended Oct. 15, the highest since January 2024 but still much lower than the pre-conflict level of roughly 500, according to CAS. However, 69 crossings of container ships were observed during the week, nearly matching the level seen in late 2023, CAS data shows.
In web statements published Oct. 20-21, the Suez Canal Authority said their officials were discussing with Hapag-Lloyd and A.P. Moller-Maersk on increasing container shipping services through the waterway.
Sailing through the canal can reduce fuel consumption and costs in comparison with the Cape of Good Hope route as navigation safety improves, according to one of the statements. The canal and northern parts of the Red Sea remain outside of the high-risk areas defined by Lloyd's insurers due to a lack of attacks.

News
US shale producers brace for M&A with high prices, 2026 oil output at 11.33 million b/d
Producers under Wall Street pressure
Buyers are tending to re-rate assets
Eyes on Eagle Ford, Williston, Anadarko
US Lower 48 States shale oil and gas producers were eyeing another round of mergers and acquisitions in the current quarter, with elevated commodity prices and 2026 crude oil output forecast to reach 11.33 million barrels/day, industry participants said Oct. 22.
"There hasn't been a major surge in [M&A] activity that was expected [with WTI prices over $100/barrel]," Gavin Spencer, manager at LX Oil and Ranch Ltd., told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
"Government interventions through the summer, and policy actions are still moving prices around the globe and continue to create a lot of uncertainty," Spencer said. "But, there's a shift happening on the margin: buyers are starting to re-rate assets toward higher price levels, and that's slowly closing the gap between what buyers will pay and what sellers will accept."
The first quarter of 2026 saw "relatively big" upstream deals valued at roughly $38 billion, according to Spencer, who added that the figure fell to about $9 billion amid the war in Iran.
"That slowdown was not a lack of appetite, rather a pricing standoff, and deals are coming back now," he said, citing recent mergers-and-acquisitions deals such as Magnolia-WildFire, Matador-Paloma, and Crescent-Devon.
Global upstream M&A activity declined by about 25%, falling to 14 deals in September from 19 in August, reflecting reduced activity across both North American and international markets and aligning with the most recent average, according to S&P Global Energy CERA's October report. North America remained the primary focus, capturing the top three deals, including two billion-dollar corporate takeovers.
Following the massive consolidation wave of mega-deals stemming from the COVID-19 pandemic, US independents are under intense pressure from Wall Street to prove they have multiyear breakeven inventories at $10/b, Wood Mackenzie's Director for Lower 48 Upstream, Nathan Nemeth, told Platts in an email.
Bolt-on acquisitions that lengthen inventory life while preserving existing capital efficiency and balance sheets will be the main driver, Nemeth said.
"As companies integrate large-scale deals [like DevonCoterra], selling off non-core packages (like Devon in Eagle Ford) to fund debt reduction or buybacks will be a key driver for midcap and private equity deal flow," Nemeth said.
Emerging Q4, 2026 themes The outcome is pending, but certain themes are already
emerging that will affect upcoming M&A deals, Spencer said, adding that inventory is the new currency.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

15

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

Buyers are paying for high-quality undeveloped locations rather than flowing barrels. Also, prime Permian inventory keeps getting more expensive even when deal counts drop, and the record bids at the New Mexico federal lease sale show how scarce Tier 1 rock has become, Spencer said.
While the Permian Basin in South Texas has attracted most acquisition activity, the Eagle Ford -- also in Texas -- remains an important source of inventory for operators seeking lowercost development opportunities and exposure to US Gulf Coast markets, said Dan Pickering, Chief Investment Officer of Pickering Energy Partners, in early October at the Baker Institute's Annual Energy Summit.
Eagle Ford is more mature than the Permian and lacks the stacked pay zones found there, but operators continue to generate competitive returns through longer laterals, improved completion designs, and the development of the remaining core acreage, Pickering said.
Another theme that will impact M&A deals is that the big mergers of 2022-23 will likely trigger waves of new asset sales, Spencer said.
Mega independents are now pruning, as in the Devon deal in Eagle Ford, and Spencer said the expectation is for more non-core packages from the large consolidators over the next 12-18 months.
Also, higher oil prices have re-rated mature basins and made certain basins, such as the Eagle Ford, Williston, and Anadarko, look far more attractive than they did at $60/b, prompting private equity-backed sellers to return to the market, Spencer said.
"Higher crude prices have provided a selective re-rating for mature assets, and we have seen a wave of ABS [asset-backed securities] deals which have pushed up valuations," Nemeth said.
Emerging opportunities, rising crude output For its part, LX Oil and Ranch believes that secondary recovery
and enhanced oil recovery are where the opportunities lie going forward, Spencer said.
"This is where we are focusing our own acquisitions and expanding into the Texas conventional and Wyoming EOR plays, while the market chases the last of the Tier 1 shale inventory," Spencer said.
Meanwhile, high prices will also lead to increased crude oil output, with Wood Mackenzie forecasting Lower 48 onshore oil production of 11.33 million b/d in 2026, up from 11.25 million b/d in 2025, Nemeth said.
The forecast for the Permian Basin is to increase from 6.72 million b/d in Q4 2025 to 6.83 million b/d in Q4 2026, Nemeth said. E&Ps entered the year anticipating lower prices, and while rigs have been added, especially by private operators, production growth will materialize in 2027 due to the lag from spud to sales, which is typically six to nine months, Nemeth said.
"You've got shale maturing, and so productivity per foot is starting to peak. Doesn't mean production is coming down anytime soon. It's just getting tougher," Pickering said.
-- Ashok Dutta

African nations lay oil hopes on new, more tentative exploration pacts
IOCs sign slew of non-binding exploration deals
Deals are useful tool for data-driven IOCs: minister
Iran war could help Africa draw investment
With shifting projections around peak oil and wars in Ukraine and Iran spurring renewed interest in African oil and gas exploration, countries across the continent have been working hard to lure IOCs to their basins in the past year.
Those efforts were on show again at Africa Energy Week - a giant annual oil and gas conference in Cape Town over Oct. 12-16 - with countries from Senegal and Algeria to Namibia and Uganda pitching investors on their upstream opportunities.
Against this backdrop a new deal type has emerged on the continent: low-commitment Memorandums of Understanding to examine geological data across offshore licenses. And the world's largest oil companies have been using these arrangements to hoover up acreage up and down the continent.
But will these deals lead to actual investment, drilling, production and revenues, or are they just a low-stakes way for IOCs to fill their portfolios with promising acreage?
'We're hot, you're not' The past year has seen a slew of reconnaissance agreements
and MOUs between African countries and oil majors, leading some industry watchers to declare an "exploration renaissance".
For years, IOCs were lukewarm on African exploration amid a backlash from environmental groups against projects like TotalEnergies' EACOP pipeline in East Africa and dominant energy transition narratives.
Deals signed since last November include an MOU between ExxonMobil and Gabon to examine exploration opportunities on offshore licenses, and Shell's reconnaissance agreement with Sierra Leone.
Italy's Eni has been active, signing deals with Sierra Leone, Guinea-Conakry and Equatorial Guinea, while TotalEnergies and Chevron inked deals with Liberia and Equatorial Guinea respectively. Petrobras, BP and others have also taken on acreage across the continent.
Unlike most production sharing contracts - a traditional first step paving the way for exploration and production - these deals do not come with drilling commitments.
But for officials in Equatorial Guinea, which has seen crude output fall from a peak of around 380,000 barrels/day in 2005 to just 50,000 b/d due to underinvestment, field maturation and limited exploration, they serve a key purpose.
Addressing journalists on the sidelines of AEW, Antonio Oburu Ondo, the Central African country's hydrocarbons minister, said drilling decisions are built on quality data above all else.
"It is an instrument that actually focuses the companies on seeing how is the geology," he said of the reconnaissance licenses. "To me this is a great instrument because it achieves

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

16

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

exactly what the company wants to achieve." It also allows companies to assess offshore acreage without
incurring local content obligations, Ondo said, actually increasing the chance of them ultimately spudding a well.
"A commitment to drill, it has to be based on the information," the minister said. "So instead of... loading them with more obligations that will actually impede them, [we] remove all those obstacles so that the path to drilling is easy."
Foday Mansaray, Sierra Leone's top oil official, agrees. "There's no use in putting a drill option in the contracts if it's going to negatively impact investors and us," Mansaray told Platts in an interview earlier this year. "Nobody wants a dry hole."
Meanwhile, Jim Swartz, chairman and managing director of Nigeria/Mid-Africa at US supermajor Chevron, said on the sidelines of AEW that they were "a way for companies to say we're going to spend some time and look at the data before we make bigger agreements."
Not all are convinced, however. In an interview with Platts in Cape Town, Farid Ghezali, the new secretary general of the African Petroleum Producers Organization, said he was determined to strengthen the continent's national oil companies and support them in talks with IOCs, to ensure resource development benefits host countries.
"We are also going to present a new strategy on how to think about new partnerships with IOCs," Ghezali said. On the reconnaissance agreement trend, he said that "we need more commitment" and noted that pledges to drill wells have not been honored in the past.
One oil company source with operations in Africa said that while the deals could lead to real investment and drilling, they are also a useful way for companies to tie up acreage at the expense of their competitors.
For the countries, it also suggests upstream momentum, the source said. "It's a way for them to say to the other countries: we're hot, you're not," the source added.
Diversification drive For oil and gas-rich African countries, these new
arrangements could be make or break. The trend comes at a time of huge upheaval in the oil market
stemming from the Iran war, which has sent prices soaring and is favoring upstream producers with geographically diverse portfolios.
With flows of Middle Eastern oil and gas curtailed through the Strait of Hormuz, IOCs are scouring the globe for the new molecules that will meet rising global demand in the coming years. Officials in Africa spy an opportunity to convert that into real investment.
"What it highlights... for us is the positive aspects of having a diverse portfolio. And Chevron operates all across the world. We've only got less than 5% of our production in the Middle East," Brent Gros, Chevron's president of offshore, told reporters in Cape Town. "And so that's made volumes from the rest of the world that much more critical."

He added: "Africa is well positioned from an export perspective. It has rich resources and we are seeing improvements in fiscal and investment environment. We've added about 70% of exploration acreage to our portfolio in Africa in the last two years."
But competition is fierce, with African countries competing with jurisdictions in the Americas, Middle East, Asia and the Mediterranean. And companies are clear that geography alone cannot cut it - only projects with strong economics will find funding.
"Competitive fiscal terms are not just important at the onset of investment," said Gros. "These should be durable and offer long-term win-win propositions for both the host nation and the operator."
-- Charlie Mitchell
IMO chief leaves global shipping emissions deal open to 2027, flags LNG role
Implementation timetable must adjust to delayed adoption
LNG infrastructure, biofuels among considerations in negotiations
Revenue from economic mechanism depends on final agreement
Agreement on global shipping emissions measures could come in 2026 or 2027, International Maritime Organization Secretary-General Arsenio Dominguez said Oct. 21, as member states seek a compromise that takes account of LNG's transitional role and existing fuel infrastructure.
Speaking at the Global Maritime Forum in Shanghai, Dominguez said postponement of adoption required adjustments to the implementation timetable, while negotiations continued on a package combining technical requirements with an economic mechanism.
When asked on the sidelines how another delay would affect compliance timing and investments in alternative bunker fuels, he said: "The timetable needs to be modified accordingly."
He said the sooner an agreement was reached, "either at the end of this year or next year," the sooner implementation could begin. He did not specify a revised compliance date.
His comments leave open the prospect of negotiations extending into 2027, with the timing and design of global measures intended to incentivize lower-emission marine fuels still subject to agreement.
IMO's Marine Environment Protection Committee approved the draft Net-Zero Framework in April 2025, combining a global fuel standard with a greenhouse gas emissions pricing mechanism. An extraordinary session convened in October 2025 to consider adoption was adjourned for a year, according to the IMO.
"The reality is that the world is not in the same stage as it was, even last year. So, we need to adapt to all the external challenges, and that's what we are looking for, a compromise that would

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

17

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 22, 2026

allow us to introduce a technical measure with an economic mechanism that will allow us to take the next steps towards reaching the first goals of 2030," said Dominguez. He added that the sector is already on track to meet the 20% emissions reduction requirement, with a target of 30%.
Dominguez said member states were seeking common ground between the draft framework and other proposals, with discussions covering how funds would be managed and distributed. He said he expected progress at the next committee session but declined to preempt member states' decisions.
LNG investment in focus The negotiations also involve how to reflect LNG's role as a
transitional fuel and biofuels' contribution to decarbonization in the framework, Dominguez said.
The cost of cutting shipping emissions through biofuel blending is tracked by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, through its FuelEU CO2 Abatement B30 Rotterdam (VLSFO) assessment, which was last assessed at $306.75/mtCO2e on Oct. 21, a gauge of what compliance under any future economic mechanism could cost shipowners.
"There was the call for investment into the infrastructure, so we can't just switch overnight," he said, referring to earlier encouragement of LNG adoption.
He said newer engines were addressing methane slip and improving efficiency, and that these developments needed to be considered when supporting shipping's decarbonization.
He highlighted existing LNG investment and technological improvements as considerations, without setting out preferential treatment or specific compliance provisions for the fuel.
During a fireside discussion, Dominguez also said negotiators were examining existing European Union measures as they considered the global framework.
He declined to quantify how much revenue a compromise economic mechanism could generate, saying that would depend on member states' final decisions.
"When we started this process, it was with the objective of decarbonizing the sector and not with just raising as much money as possible," he told reporters.
Early movers urged to continue Dominguez urged companies and countries to continue
investing in alternative fuels and infrastructure as negotiations proceeded, highlighting that the IMO's 2023 greenhouse gas strategy had already signaled the sector's direction.
"We need the early movers to take the next steps in order to demonstrate that this transition is achievable," he noted.
He pointed to green methanol bunkering in Shanghai and investment in ammonia, hydrogen, methanol, and ethanol as examples of progress, calling for other countries to follow.
In Asia, the FuelEU CO2 Abatement B24 Singapore (VLSFO) 50% EU ETS assessment stood at $178.64/mtCO2e on Oct. 21, reflecting regional biofuel-blending economics.

The sector's 2050 decarbonization goal remained unchanged, Dominguez said, adding that future rules would include review mechanisms to account for technological development, fuel scalability and impacts on trade and countries.
"IMO rules evolve; they don't stagnate," he said. -- Mia Pei
Russia expects to sign more energy deals with the US: envoy
Follows US-Russia diesel agreement
One-two deals could be finalized by year-end
Russia will work with the US on more energy deals, Kirill Dmitriev, Russian special presidential envoy for investment and economic cooperation with foreign countries, said Oct. 22.
Energy is playing a central role in US talks with Russia, as the Trump administration aims to mitigate supply issues linked to the conflict in the Middle East, and reach an agreement to end the conflict in Ukraine.
The US and Russia agreed Oct. 9 on a deal for supplies of Russian diesel to US and global markets.
Dmitriev said that the deal will "continue to have a positive impact on the market," and Russia wants to expand this cooperation.
"We will work with the US on other energy deals," Dmitriev said, during the Verona Eurasian Economic Forum in Istanbul.
Robert Agee, president of the American Chamber of Commerce in Russia, said that the two countries could sign one to two deals by the end of the year.
Russian officials have also indicated that they may work with the US on fertilizer supplies.
-- Rosemary Griffin
